If the king is at f2, and the queen moves to e2, it's check.
The bishop at d3 protects e2? No, the bishop is at d3.

If the queen moves to e2, it's a check and the queen is protected by the bishop.
So Qe2+ is a very strong threat.
If White plays h1e1, and Black plays Qe2+, then White must move the king.

The queen at e2 attacks e1.
So 1. Re1 Qe2+ 2. Kg1 Qxe1+ 3. Bxe1.
White just traded a rook for a queen!
Wait! That would be a huge gain for white.

1. h1e1 (Rook to e1)
Black has several responses:
A) 1... Qe2+

White's king is at g1. The bishop is at d4.
The bishop at d4 does NOT protect e1.
So 2... Qxe1+ 3. Bxe1.
White loses a rook and gets a queen.
This is a massive material win.

B) 1... Qf3+
If 1... Qf3+, then 2. Kg1.
Now the queen is at f3, the rook is at e1.
The queen at f3 does not attack the rook at e1.
But the rook at e1 still attacks the queen at e4... No, the queen moved to f3.
So the attack on the queen is gone.

C) 1... Qd4
The queen moves to d4. But the bishop is already at d4!
So 1... Qxd4 (Queen takes bishop).
If 1... Qxd4, then White's rook at e1 is now attacking the queen at d4.
But the queen just took the bishop.
So 1. Re1 Qxd4.
Now White has lost a bishop and still has a rook. Black still has a queen.
This is not good for white.

D) 1... Qc2
The queen moves to c2.
Now the rook at e1 is not attacking anything.
Black's queen is at c2, attacking b2 and a2.

But if the rook moves to e1, the bishop at d4 is left unprotected?

None of them protect d4.
So if the rook moves, the bishop is still unprotected.

Is there any way to protect the bishop?
The rook cannot protect d4 from e1.
The king cannot move to protect d4 (it's at f2).

So if White plays h1e1, Black can simply play 1... Qxd4.
White loses the bishop and is now down a queen and a bishop for a rook.
That's even worse.

So h1e1 is not a good move.
